[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6f9cd7","wall_time_seconds":0.6723662,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":8768,"output":"PT157-S3-Q15 C Ants sometimes live in hollow places in the roots of a certain orchid species. Those orchids of that species that house ants are far healthier than those that do not. Since the ants store organic matter in the orchids\' roots, an explanation for the superior health of the orchids that house ants is that the organic matter stored by the ants provides those orchids with additional nutrients. Which one of the following, if true, is an alternative explanation for the better health of orchids that house the ants? {\'A\': \\"Microscopic organisms that are present in the orchids\' roots break down the organic matter stored there by the ants.\\", \'B\': \'The nutrients present in the organic matter stored by the ants are the same as those present in the soil in which the orchids are found.\', \'C\': \\"The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids prey on an insect species whose members eat the orchids\' roots.\\", \'D\': \'The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids do not play a role in pollinating the flowers of the plant.\', \'E\': \'Most plant species whose roots, stems, or leaves harbor insects are more prone to disease and rot than are other plants.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Curious Fact\', \\"Why are the ant-hosting orchids of a given species healthier than the orchids that don\'t host ants?\\"), (\'Provided Explanation\', \\"The ants store organic matter in the orchids\' roots. That matter gives the orchids extra nutrients, and the extra nutrients make them healthier.\\"), (\'Goal\', \'Our task is an alternate explanation for the correlation between housing ants and being a healthier orchid. We are not asked to attack the nutrient story, only to give another story that would also explain the fact.\\\\n\\\\nWhenever we need an alternate explanation for a correlation, check the usual routes.\\\\n\\\\nReverse cause: maybe the healthier orchids come first, and ants prefer to move into healthy roots.\\\\n\\\\nThird factor: maybe something that comes with the ants, such as a damp spot in the soil, is what makes those orchids healthier.\\\\n\\\\nSame cause, different mechanism: maybe the ants do help the orchids, just not through nutrients. They could protect the roots, for example.\\\\n\\\\nSo look for an answer that explains why ant-housing orchids are healthier without relying on the stored organic matter. An answer that only makes the nutrient story weaker, or adds detail to it, is not an alternative.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \\"Microscopic organisms breaking down the stored organic matter is a detail of how the nutrients might reach the orchid. That fills in the author\'s own explanation rather than replacing it. We need a different reason the orchids are healthier.\\"), (\'B\', \'B is tempting because it makes the nutrient story look shakier: the stored matter has the same nutrients as the soil. But the stem asks for an alternative explanation, and B gives no other reason ant-housing orchids are healthier. Casting doubt on one explanation is not offering another.\'), (\'C\', \'C keeps the ants as the cause but changes how they help. The ants \\"prey on an insect species whose members eat the orchids’ roots.\\" Orchids with ants lose fewer roots to those insects, so they are healthier for a reason that has nothing to do with stored nutrients. That is a full second explanation for the same fact.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: on alternate-explanation questions, look for a different path from the same two facts, here the ants and the orchids\\\\\' health.\'), (\'D\', \\"D tells us one thing the ants don\'t do: they don\'t pollinate the flowers. A thing the ants don\'t do cannot explain why their orchids are healthier. D removes one possible explanation and offers nothing in its place.\\"), (\'E\', \'E points the wrong way: plants that harbor insects tend to be more prone to disease and rot. That would make the ant-housing orchids\\\\\' good health more surprising, not less. It is also about \\"Most plant species,\\" not this orchid. No explanation here.\')]\\r\\nPT157-S3-Q25 A To test the claim that vitamin C is effective in treating acne, scientists administered it to one group of subjects and a placebo to a control group. The group receiving vitamin C had less severe acne during the study than did the control group. It was subsequently discovered, however, that half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell, no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups. Therefore, we can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne. Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument? {\'A\': \'The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.\', \'B\': \'None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.\', \'C\': \\"During the study, the severity of the subjects\' acne was lower than the national average.\\", \'D\': \'Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.\', \'E\': \'Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Opposing Evidence\', \'During a study, a group of subjects receiving vitamin C had less severe acne than did the control group, which received a placebo.\'), (\'Evidence\', \'Half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell [which pill they were given], no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups.\'), (\'Conclusion\', \'We can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.\'), (\'Evaluate\', \'Let\\\\\'s be clear about which groups are being compared. Overall, the vitamin C group had less severe acne than the placebo group. Then it turned out that half of each group knew which pill they had, and half could not tell.\\\\n\\\\nAmong the subjects who could not tell, there was no difference in acne. So the overall gap came from the subjects who knew. The author\\\\\'s reading: knowing you got the vitamin made the difference, not the vitamin. The blind comparison is treated as the fair test, and that test found nothing.\\\\n\\\\nThat reading depends on the blind comparison really being fair. Finding \\"no difference in the severity of acne\\" means both blind groups ended up in the same place during the study. It only shows the vitamin did nothing if both groups started in the same place too.\\\\n\\\\nGiven that, how could we argue vitamin C did help? Suppose the vitamin C subjects started with worse acne. Then ending up equal to the placebo group means they improved more. The \\"no difference\\" result would hide a real benefit.\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer showing the blind comparison was not a fair one: most likely, that the vitamin C subjects started out with worse acne than the placebo subjects, so matching them during the study is evidence of improvement. More generally, any answer giving evidence that vitamin C actually helped the blind subjects will weaken.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'The conclusion rests on the blind comparison showing \\"no difference in the severity of acne\\" between the groups. A says the vitamin C subjects \\"had a history of suffering from more severe acne\\" than the placebo subjects. If the vitamin C group started worse and ended up equal, it improved more than the placebo group did. That is a benefit, hidden inside a result of no difference.\\\\n\\\\nThe reusable test for study arguments: before trusting a comparison, ask whether the two groups started out alike. If they didn\\\\\'t, equal results can mean unequal effects.\'), (\'B\', \\"B rules out vitamin C subjects taking extra doses on their own. That closes a loophole, but it doesn\'t make the blind comparison less trustworthy, and it gives no evidence that the vitamin worked. The no-difference result among blind subjects stands as it was.\\"), (\'C\', \\"C describes the subjects as a whole: their acne was below the national average. The argument compares the groups inside the study, so a fact about the whole pool doesn\'t change that comparison. C gives no reason to think the vitamin C group would have been worse off without the pills.\\"), (\'D\', \'D is the most tempting trap. If placebo subjects got vitamin C from food, the control group was not vitamin-free. But D says only that \\"Some\\" placebo subjects ate vitamin C-rich foods, which is an ordinary diet and could just as easily describe the vitamin C group. Nothing suggests the food matched the pill\\\\\'s dose or that it happened among the blind subjects. So D barely dents the comparison, while A gives direct evidence that the vitamin helped.\'), (\'E\', \'Some subjects who knew they had placebos skipped them. Those subjects are in the half that knew, which the conclusion already sets aside. And a placebo has no active ingredient, so taking it or not changes nothing about vitamin C. The blind comparison is untouched.\')]\\r\\nPT157-S3-Q6 C Winchester Township cannot afford to keep its several small branch libraries supplied with a wide selection of current books. However, if the branch libraries were closed, then Winchester could afford to open one large library, which could carry broader and more current selections. Thus, Winchester would better meet its residents\' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library. Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the argument above? {\'A\': \'Many Winchester residents never use the branch libraries because of the irregular hours each branch keeps.\', \'B\': \'Most Winchester residents have complained about the selection of books at the branch libraries.\', \'C\': \'The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.\', \'D\': \'It would cost Winchester a significant amount of money to build a larger library.\', \'E\': \\"Each of Winchester\'s branch libraries attempts to cater to the tastes of the residents of the entire township.\\"}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Evidence\', \\"Winchester can\'t afford to keep its several small branch libraries supplied with a wide selection of current books. If the branch libraries were closed, then Winchester could afford to open one large library. The large library could carry broader and more current selections [of books].\\"), (\'Conclusion\', \\"Winchester would better meet its residents\' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library.\\"), (\'Evaluate\', \\"The conclusion makes a recommendation about building a new library. It does this by introducing a new concept of better meeting residents\' needs. A couple of assumptions are required to reach this conclusion. First, the argument assumes that one large library would better meet residents\' needs than several smaller ones. We can easily envision a situation where it\'s convenient for residents to visit the branch libraries that are close to their neighborhoods, while a single main library would be farther away and therefore less accessible. The argument also assumes that a broader and more current selection of books would better meet residents\' needs. But people use libraries for many reasons other than borrowing books. Libraries often have computers, study rooms, and meeting rooms that residents can use. Libraries often offer classes and events. And people are likely to borrow older books from libraries, not just current ones.\\\\n\\\\nSo, given that one big library would have better books, how could we argue residents would be worse off? Show that the new library would fail them in some other way, most likely because they could not easily get to it.\\"), (\'Goal\', \'A correct answer is likely to focus on one of these unintended consequences of closing the branches, a cost that would stop one large library from serving residents better than the existing branches. The likeliest target: residents could not easily reach the single new library.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'Residents who never use the branches because of irregular hours lose nothing when the branches close. If anything, A gives another reason the branches are failing residents. It shows no way the single large library would serve them worse.\'), (\'B\', \\"Complaints about the book selection at the branches back up the argument\'s premise that the branches are poorly supplied. That makes a bigger, better-stocked library sound more welcome, so B strengthens rather than weakens.\\"), (\'C\', \'The argument assumes better books means better service. C says \\"The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.\\" A library most residents can\\\\\'t easily reach does not meet their needs, however good its books are. The branches, poorly stocked as they are, at least sit nearby.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: when an argument recommends a plan for one benefit, look for the cost it ignored.\'), (\'D\', \'The argument already says closing the branches would let Winchester afford the large library. A \\"significant amount of money\\" is consistent with that. D would weaken only if the cost exceeded what Winchester could afford, and it doesn\\\\\'t say so.\'), (\'E\', \\"If each branch tries to serve the whole township, the branches duplicate each other\'s job, which makes one central library sound sensible. E gives no reason the residents would lose anything by consolidating, so it does not weaken.\\")]\\r\\nPT158-S2-Q21 B A geologist recently claimed to have discovered in clay a previously unknown form of life: \\"nanobes,\\" one-tenth the size of the smallest known bacteria. However, it is unlikely that nanobes truly are living things. They are probably inanimate artifacts of the clay\'s microscopic structure, because a nanobe is too small to contain a reproductive mechanism, a prerequisite for life. Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument? {\'A\': \'No known form of bacteria is complicated enough in structure to engage in a sexual type of reproduction.\', \'B\': \'Single-celled creatures can combine to form a multicelled structure and then reproduce before they disband into separate single cells again.\', \'C\': \'The material phenomena that some scientists claim are the fossilized remains of bacteria in meteorites from Mars are approximately the same size as nanobes.\', \'D\': \'Previous definitions of life were based on research done with inferior microscopes no longer in use.\', \'E\': \'Animals such as cold-blooded lizards can be physiologically simpler, though still larger, than other animals.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Conclusion (unlikely = opinion)\', \\"It\'s unlikely that nanobes are living things.\\"), (\'Intermediate Conclusion\', \\"Nanobes are probably inanimate artifacts of the microscopic structure of the clay in which they\'re found.\\"), (\'Evidence (because)\', \'Nanobes are 1/10 the size of the smallest known bacteria. They are too small to contain a reproductive mechanism, which is a prerequisite for life.\'), (\'Evaluate\', \'The whole argument rests on a rule and a fact. Rule: anything alive has a reproductive mechanism. Fact: a single nanobe is too small to contain one. Put them together and nanobes can\\\\\'t be alive, so the geologist must be looking at bits of clay.\\\\n\\\\nGiven that a single nanobe is too small to hold a reproductive mechanism, how could we still argue that nanobes are living things? We can\\\\\'t deny the rule; it\\\\\'s a premise. So look at what the author assumed about how the rule applies. The author assumes the mechanism has to fit inside one nanobe. What if nanobes don\\\\\'t reproduce one at a time? If several could team up, the group could have the mechanism that no single nanobe can hold.\\\\n\\\\nA second route goes after the author\\\\\'s alternative: anything that makes \\"inanimate artifacts of the clay\\\\\'s microscopic structure\\" a poor explanation. That one is harder to predict, so expect the first route.\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer showing that a living thing can reproduce without each individual containing the whole mechanism, for example by joining up with others. Less likely: an answer that makes the clay-artifact explanation hard to believe.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'A is about sexual reproduction in bacteria. The argument never said life requires sexual reproduction, only some reproductive mechanism, and bacteria reproduce in other ways. A says nothing about whether something one-tenth the size of a bacterium can reproduce, so the size problem is untouched.\'), (\'B\', \'The evidence only works if each nanobe has to carry its own reproductive mechanism. B breaks that assumption: \\"Single-celled creatures can combine to form a multicelled structure and then reproduce.\\" Apply that to nanobes. One nanobe may be too small, but a group could reproduce together, so being tiny no longer rules out life.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: when you can\\\\\'t deny a premise, ask what the author assumed about how it applies, then find the answer that breaks the assumption. Here it was one individual, one mechanism.\'), (\'C\', \'C gives us material phenomena about the size of nanobes, but only says some scientists \\"claim\\" they are fossilized bacteria. Something nanobe-sized that we knew was alive would weaken badly. A disputed fossil isn\\\\\'t that, and it doesn\\\\\'t show anything that small ever had a reproductive mechanism.\'), (\'D\', \'D attacks old definitions of life built with \\"inferior microscopes no longer in use.\\" The argument relies on one requirement, a reproductive mechanism, and D never says that requirement is wrong. The old definitions may have demanded the same thing. Nothing here helps a nanobe reproduce.\'), (\'E\', \\"E says some animals are larger but simpler than others, so size and complexity don\'t line up perfectly. It runs the wrong way for us: we need something tiny that is still complex enough to reproduce, not something big and simple. And one fact about lizards says nothing about the scale of nanobes.\\")]\\r\\nPT158-S2-Q7 C Researcher: In a recent study of elementary school computers, we found that all keyboards and most monitors were positioned higher than recommended for children. Consequently, children were seated in ways that encouraged craned necks, awkwardly placed wrists, and other unhealthy postures. Evidently, most elementary school computers are installed without consideration of their effect on posture, and thus put children at the same risk for repetitive stress injuries as office workers. Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the researcher\'s argument? {\'A\': \'The recommended height for computers is different for children than for adults.\', \'B\': \'Children spend more time working with computers at home than at school.\', \'C\': \\"The greater suppleness of children\'s bodies makes them less susceptible than adults to repetitive stress injuries.\\", \'D\': \\"Office workers\' keyboards and monitors are usually not at the recommended heights for healthy postures for adults.\\", \'E\': \'Office workers are more likely to report injuries than children are.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Conclusion (thus)\', \'Children are put at the same risk for repetitive stress injuries as office workers.\'), (\'Intermediate Conclusion (Evidently)\', \\"Most elementary school computers are installed without consideration of their effect on students\' posture.\\"), (\'Evidence\', \'In a study, all the keyboards and most monitors were placed higher than recommended for children, leading children to sit in ways that encourage craned necks, awkward wrists, and other unhealthy postures.\'), (\'Evaluate\', \'The argument makes two jumps, so there are two places to push.\\\\n\\\\nJump one: the computers are set too high, so they were installed \\"without consideration of their effect on posture.\\" Given that all the keyboards and most monitors sit too high for children, how could someone still have considered posture? Maybe the computers are shared with adults and the height is a compromise. Possible, but a stretch.\\\\n\\\\nJump two is weaker. Given that children sit in unhealthy postures, how could they still not face \\"the same risk\\" as office workers? \\"Same\\" is a strong word. It assumes a child\\\\\'s body takes the same damage from bad posture that an adult\\\\\'s does, and that the exposure is comparable. Office workers sit at keyboards all day; children at school probably don\\\\\'t. And a child\\\\\'s body may handle strain differently. Either difference breaks \\"the same risk.\\"\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer that makes children different from office workers in a way that matters for repetitive stress injuries: less time at the computer, or bodies that handle the strain better. A less likely route: a good reason the computers were set high even though posture was considered.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'A says the recommended height differs for children and adults. The researcher already relied on that: the computers were \\"higher than recommended for children.\\" A just explains why they count as too high, so it fits the evidence instead of challenging the jump to the same risk as office workers.\'), (\'B\', \\"B is about time at home, but the conclusion is about the risk school computers create. More home use doesn\'t make the school postures any less harmful, and home setups could be just as bad. The impact is unclear, so B can\'t seriously weaken.\\"), (\'C\', \'The researcher moves from bad posture straight to the same risk as office workers. That only works if a child\\\\\'s body reacts to strain the way an adult\\\\\'s does. C denies it: children\\\\\'s \\"greater suppleness\\" makes them \\"less susceptible than adults\\" to these injuries. Same postures, lower risk, so \\"the same risk\\" fails.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: when a conclusion says two groups are equal, look for a relevant difference between them.\'), (\'D\', \\"D says office workers\' equipment is usually at the wrong height too. That makes the two groups look more alike, which helps the comparison. To weaken, we need a difference between children and office workers, and D supplies a similarity.\\"), (\'E\', \\"E is about who reports injuries, not who gets them. Children might report fewer injuries simply because they don\'t recognize them or don\'t tell anyone. Reporting rates don\'t show their actual risk is lower, and the conclusion is about risk.\\")]\\r\\nPT158-S3-Q24 D Scientist: Some consumer groups claim that the economic benefits of genetically engineered foodstuffs may be offset by hidden health risks to humans. However, the risk is minimal. In most cases of deliberate alteration of a plant\'s genetic structure only a single gene in about 750,000 has been changed. Since the change in the organism\'s genetic structure is so slight, it cannot have effects significant enough to be worrisome. Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the scientist\'s argument? {\'A\': \'The genetically engineered plants that have been developed so far have few advantages over plants that are not genetically engineered.\', \'B\': \\"Whatever health risks there are in food from genetically altered plants may be somewhat reduced by other factors such as enrichment of the plants\' vitamin and mineral content.\\", \'C\': \'Scientists have yet to determine, for each characteristic of some plants and animals used for food, the precise location of the genes that determine that characteristic.\', \'D\': \'There are plants that are known to be toxic to some animals and whose toxicity is known to be affected by the alteration of a single gene.\', \'E\': \'Research has shown that those consumers who are most strongly opposed to genetically altered foods tend to be ill-informed on the issue.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Conclusion\', \'The health risks of GMO foods are minimal.\'), (\'Intermediate Conclusion\', \\"A GMO food can\'t have effects that would be significant enough to be worrisome.\\"), (\'Evidence (since)\', \\"In most cases of deliberate alteration of a plant\'s genes, only a single gene in 750,000 has been changed. This change is very slight.\\"), (\'Evaluate\', \'There are two moves here, and we can push on either.\\\\n\\\\nMove one, in the last sentence: the change is slight, so it \\"cannot have effects significant enough to be worrisome.\\" Does that follow? Pull the wrong block from a Jenga tower and the whole thing comes down. What matters is not how much changed but what the changed part does. If a single gene controls something important, changing it could matter a lot.\\\\n\\\\nMove two: \\"In most cases\\" only one gene in about 750,000 is changed, so the risk is minimal. Most cases aren\\\\\'t all cases. What if the foods people worry about are the ones where many genes were changed?\\\\n\\\\nMove one is the bigger target. \\"Cannot\\" is a claim about every slight change, so a single counterexample hurts it.\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer showing that changing a single gene can have a significant, worrying effect. Less likely: an answer showing that engineered foods often involve more than a slight change, or direct evidence that their health risks are serious.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \\"A says engineered plants offer few advantages over ordinary ones. That\'s about benefits, and the scientist\'s conclusion is only about risk. A plant can be pointless and perfectly safe. A gives no reason to think a one-gene change can do harm.\\"), (\'B\', \'B says whatever risks exist \\"may be somewhat reduced by other factors\\" such as added vitamins and minerals. That makes the risk smaller, if anything, which fits the claim that \\"the risk is minimal.\\" A weakener needs the risk to be bigger than the scientist thinks.\'), (\'C\', \\"C says scientists haven\'t located the genes behind every trait. That is uncertainty, and it cuts both ways: the changed gene might matter or might not. It never shows a one-gene change can have a significant effect. D gives a known case; C only gives a gap in knowledge.\\"), (\'D\', \'The scientist\\\\\'s key move is that a change this slight \\"cannot have effects significant enough to be worrisome.\\" D gives a counterexample: plants whose toxicity \\"is known to be affected by the alteration of a single gene.\\" D speaks of toxicity to \\"some animals,\\" not specifically to humans, but that is still enough to break \\"cannot\\": one gene changed, and something as serious as toxicity changed with it. The size of a change doesn\\\\\'t decide its effect; what the gene does decides it.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: when an argument says something is too small to matter, look for a case where something that small mattered a lot.\'), (\'E\', \\"E attacks the opponents: the consumers most opposed tend to be ill-informed. Even if the critics are wrong about everything, the scientist\'s own reasoning could still be bad. Undercutting the other side does nothing to the step from a slight change to no worrying effect.\\")]\\r\\nPT158-S3-Q5 C Domesticated animals, such as dogs, have come into existence by the breeding of only the individuals of a wild species that are sufficiently tame. For example, if when breeding wolves one breeds only those that display tameness when young, then after a number of generations the offspring will be a species of dog. Therefore, all animals can, in principle, be bred for domesticity. Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the argument? {\'A\': \'Domesticated animals cannot be turned into wild species by breeding only those animals that display some wild characteristics.\', \'B\': \'In some animal species, wild members mate more frequently than tame members.\', \'C\': \'In some animal species, no members ever display tameness.\', \'D\': \'In some animal species, tame members are less fertile than wild members.\', \'E\': \'In some domesticated animal species, some members are much more tame than other members.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Conclusion (Therefore)\', \'All animals can, in principle, be bred for domesticity.\'), (\'Evidence\', \'Domesticated animals have come into existence by breeding only the tamest individuals of a wild species. If you breed only the individuals that show tameness when young, then after a number of generations you end up with a domesticated version of the animal, as with wolves becoming dogs.\'), (\'Evaluate\', \'The conclusion is huge: \\"all animals\\" can be bred for domesticity. The evidence is one method and one example, wolves. The method needs a starting point: individuals that show tameness when young.\\\\n\\\\nSo how could we argue that not all animals can be bred for domesticity? Find a species where the method can\\\\\'t start. Say we try it with hyenas, and no hyena pup in any litter ever shows tameness. There\\\\\'s nothing to select, so there\\\\\'s no way to keep breeding tame with tame.\\\\n\\\\nNotice \\"in principle.\\" The conclusion isn\\\\\'t saying domestication is easy or fast. A slow, difficult breeding program still counts. To weaken, we need the method to be impossible for some species, not just harder.\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer saying some species have no members that ever display tameness, or anything else that makes the breeding method impossible, not merely slower, for at least one kind of animal.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'A is about the reverse trip: turning domesticated animals back into wild ones. The conclusion is only about breeding toward domesticity. Whether the trip back works says nothing about whether every species can make the trip forward.\'), (\'B\', \'B says wild members mate more often than tame ones in some species. That slows the program, but a breeder chooses which animals breed, so the tame ones can still be paired. The conclusion only claims domestication is possible \\"in principle,\\" and B doesn\\\\\'t make it impossible.\'), (\'C\', \'The method only works if some individuals \\"display tameness when young.\\" C removes that starting point for some species: \\"no members ever display tameness.\\" No tame animals means nothing to select and nothing to breed, so those species can\\\\\'t be domesticated this way, and \\"all animals\\" fails.\\\\n\\\\nThe move to repeat: when a conclusion says all, one counterexample weakens it. Find what the method requires, then look for a case where the requirement is missing.\'), (\'D\', \'D is the tempting one, since tame members being \\"less fertile\\" sounds like a real obstacle. But less fertile still means fertile. The tame animals still have offspring, so the program just takes longer, and the conclusion only says domestication is possible \\"in principle.\\" D would work only if tame members couldn\\\\\'t reproduce at all.\'), (\'E\', \'E is about species already domesticated: some members are tamer than others. That is variation after the process has succeeded. It tells us nothing about a wild species that has no tame members to start with.\')]\\r\\nPT158-S4-Q24 D Consumer advocate: Some agricultural crops are now being genetically engineered to produce important pharmaceuticals. However, this development raises the possibility that the drugs will end up in the general food supply, since if pollen from a drug-producing crop drifts into a nearby field in which an ordinary, non-drug-producing crop of the same species is being grown, the pollen could fertilize that crop and turn it into a drug-producing crop as well. Which one of the following, if true, would most weaken the consumer advocate\'s argument? {\'A\': \'As far as scientists know, none of the pharmaceuticals produced by genetically engineered crops would present any danger to public health if they were present in the general food supply.\', \'B\': \'If pollen from a genetically engineered crop is prevented from drifting into fields in which ordinary crops of the same species are being grown, then there is no risk of the pollen fertilizing the latter crops.\', \'C\': \'The genetically engineered crops that produce pharmaceuticals are not among the crop species that comprise the largest portions of the general food supply.\', \'D\': \'In crops genetically engineered to produce pharmaceuticals, the drugs are not present in any part of the plant used for food in ordinary crops of the same species.\', \'E\': \'If pollen from a drug-producing crop turned an ordinary crop of the same species into a drug-producing crop, it would be possible for scientists to identify the latter crop.\'}\\r\\nSTEPS [(\'Conclusion\', \'The fact that some crops are being genetically engineered to produce important drugs raises the possibility that these drugs will end up in the general food supply.\'), (\'Evidence\', \'If pollen from a drug-producing crop drifts into a nearby field of ordinary crops of the same species, the pollen could fertilize that ordinary crop and turn it into a drug-producing crop.\'), (\'Evaluate\', \'The consumer advocate\\\\\'s worry is a chain of links:\\\\n\\\\ndrug crop → pollen drifts into a nearby field of the same species\\\\npollen fertilizes the ordinary crop → ordinary crop becomes drug-producing\\\\ndrug-producing crop is harvested for food → drug ends up in the food supply\\\\n\\\\nThe stimulus describes how the first two links could happen. The last link is simply assumed. A plant that produces a drug somewhere in its tissue is not the same as a drug in the food we take from that plant.\\\\n\\\\nGiven that the pollen could convert a neighboring crop, how could we argue the drug still won\\\\\'t reach the food supply? Show that the converted crop\\\\\'s food part wouldn\\\\\'t carry the drug.\\\\n\\\\nAlso notice how mild the conclusion is: it only says the development \\"raises the possibility\\" of drugs in the food supply. To weaken it, an answer has to cut a link in the chain. Making the outcome less harmful, less widespread, or easier to deal with afterward leaves the possibility standing.\'), (\'Goal\', \'Look for an answer that breaks a link in the chain, most likely the last one: even if an ordinary crop turns into a drug-producing crop, the drug never gets into the food.\\\\n\\\\nSkip answers about how dangerous the drugs are, how much of the food supply is involved, or what we could do after contamination. None of those touch whether the drugs could get in.\')]\\r\\nNOTES [(\'A\', \'This lowers the stakes, not the chance. The conclusion is only that the drugs might \\"end up in the general food supply.\\" Harmless drugs in our food are still drugs in our food, so the possibility the advocate raised is untouched.\'), (\'B\', \\"This is a conditional: no drift means no risk. But nothing tells us drift is actually being prevented, so the risk the advocate describes is still there. If anything, it confirms that drift is the route to contamination, which is the advocate\'s own story.\\"), (\'C\', \'Smaller crops are still part of the food supply. The advocate never claimed the drugs would reach the biggest crops, only that they could end up in \\"the general food supply.\\" Shrinking how much food is involved doesn\\\\\'t break any link in the chain.\'), (\'D\', \'This breaks the last link. Grant everything the advocate says: the pollen drifts, fertilizes the ordinary crop, and turns it into a drug-producing crop. The drugs are still \\"not present in any part of the plant used for food,\\" so harvesting that crop for food puts no drug into the food supply. The move to repeat: map the chain from cause to feared result, then find the answer that blocks a link the author never supported.\'), (\'E\', \\"Being able to identify a converted crop is not the same as keeping it out of the food supply. The explanations themselves call these Strengthen, and the PT102 key is argued as a necessary assumption.\\r\\n  - PT143-S3-Q12 (\\"justifies the application of the policy\\") is Principle, applying a stated policy.\\r\\n- **Choice-text fidelity:** no misquoted choices found in the picked notes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Weaken-Evaluate\\r\\n\\r\\n### Weaken-Evaluate: Fidelity problems found (Weaken)\\r\\n- PT139-S4-Q7 (D note): \\"The author is promising that we can find an audio version\\" should read \\"is not promising\\".\\r\\n- PT142-S2-Q20: the Goal says the computer may have generated \\"tons of false negatives\\", and the C note says \\"the doctor won when it came to false negatives\\". Both should be false positives (the cardiologist was better on cases with no heart attack). Not picked.\\r\\n- PT106-S2-Q25 (Evaluate step): \\"Given that ... her blinking was voluntary\\" should be her humming, and involuntary. Missing spaces too (\\"it\'snotinvoluntary\\", \\"Marianneshouldbe\\"). Not picked.\\r\\n- PT119-S4-Q17: the causal-chain diagram is garbled. The C note relies on outside chemistry. The B note admits it uses outside knowledge.\\r\\n- PT109-S4-Q19: \\"FOOTNOTE:\\" pasted mid-sentence in the B note.\\r\\n- PT106-S3-Q11 (C note): the worked-number table is flattened and unreadable. PT154-S4-Q22 (C note): \\"spots\\" should be \\"sports\\". PT103-S3-Q24 (D note): \\"cars told\\" should be \\"cars sold\\".\\r\\n- PT105-S4-Q5 (D note): \\"as this correct answer shows\\" refers to B, not D.\\r\\n- Tone flags: PT146-S2-Q22, PT120-S1-Q22, PT144-S4-Q23 (disqualifying). PT154-S4-Q22 D (\\"legendarily horrid correct answer\\") and PT140-S2-Q12 C (\\"His name is Greg and he sucks\\") are milder.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### Weaken-Evaluate: Fidelity problems found (Evaluate)\\r\\n- PT139-S1-Q5 (E note): describes a different choice. Not used for the key, but it should not be copied.\\r\\n- PT148-S4-Q22 (A note): applies a Necessary Assumption rule on an Evaluate question. Broader Weak/Watch, step 4 and source disputes remain open.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Repo housekeeping\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"8bc309","wall_time_seconds":0.3565379,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2345,"output":"---\\ntype: survey\\nsubject: LR explanation data problems found during the revamp survey\\nupdated: 2026-09-27\\nstatus: read-only survey; nothing was changed\\n---\\n\\n# Side issues: data, labels and transcription\\n\\nFound while surveying the DB dump (the read-only DB dump of 2026-09-26) against the question bank (`lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`). Companion to [[revamp-list]] and [[choice-note-issues]]. Ordered by how much a student could be misled.\\n\\n## 1. Content that belongs to a different question\\n\\nThese show a student reasoning that does not match the question in front of them.\\n\\n| Question | What is wrong | Evidence |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| PT108-S3-Q6 | The DB stimulus and all five notes are for a different question from the official one. | DB stimulus: the bass catch downstream of the power plant fell. Bank stimulus for PT108-S3-Q6: the algae population downstream *rose*, with choices about detergents, water temperature and dissolved oxygen. The notes talk about predators, anglers and cooler water, which match neither set of choices. DB key C and bank key C agree only by coincidence. Needs a source check before anything else. |\\n| PT137-S3-Q25 | Evaluate step is from another question. | \\"Denise and Reshmi agree that people decide whether to commit a crime.\\" The speakers are Graham and Adelaide (chess computer). |\\n| PT143-S4-Q18 | Speaker table and Evaluate are from PT137-S3-Q25\'s template. | Table headed \\"Graham / Adelaide\\" (speakers are Wong and Tate); Evaluate says they disagree about countries being \\"better off as democracies\\" (the issue is autocracies). |\\n| PT135-S4-Q25 | A note is from another question. | \\"Neither Samuel nor Tova address a general trend of modern life.\\" Speakers are Lutsina and Priscilla. |\\n| PT133-S3-Q4 | E note carries speakers from PT142-S2-Q7. | \\"Neither Talbert nor Sklar would agree with this statement.\\" Speakers are Sam and Meli. |\\n\\n## 2. Suspected mis-keys\\n\\nNo explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. The scan\'s 16 \\"key conflict\\" hits in notes were all \\"Yes, the author assumes this, but...\\" openings. Two related items:\\n\\n- **PT158-S4-Q22** (Weaken, d5): the breakdown\'s Goal predicts the \\"different epistemologists contradict each other\\" objection, which is choice D. The key (E, \\"much debate as to which thinkers were epistemologists\\") is right, and the E note explains it well, but the breakdown steers students to D and the D note then calls contradiction \\"a feature, not a bug\\". Rewrite the breakdown.\\n- **Bank answer keys disagree with the DB on 18 questions**, all of them bank entries filed under a different PT, section or question number (\\"moved\\" in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder)). I read five of these (PT116-S2-Q6, Q7, Q8; PT131-S3-Q16, Q17) and the DB key is correct in every case. So the bank\'s `correctAnswer` is wrong for these, not the DB. Anything that reads the bank\'s key (drills, analytics, the golden-survey inventory) would mis-grade them:\\n\\n  PT101-S2-Q19 (DB E, bank D), PT101-S3-Q19 (D/E), PT113-S3-Q21 (C/A), PT113-S3-Q22 (D/A), PT113-S3-Q23 (B/A), PT113-S3-Q24 (E/A), PT116-S2-Q4 (B/C), PT116-S2-Q6 (E/B), PT116-S2-Q7 (C/D), PT116-S2-Q8 (C/E), PT119-S2-Q19 (B/E), PT119-S2-Q21 (A/C), PT119-S4-Q8 (A/C), PT129-S3-Q18 (B/D), PT129-S3-Q19 (D/A), PT131-S3-Q16 (E/C), PT131-S3-Q17 (A/E), PT148-S4-Q24 (E/A).\\n\\n  Note PT113-S3-Q21 to Q24 all have bank key A, which looks like a placeholder.\\n\\n## 3. Bank alignment\\n\\n- **PT119-S2-Q19** is matched to bank entry PT103-S2-Q24, which is a different question on the same topic (musical intervals; the bank one is about an isolated tribe\'s preferences). Its difficulty and features in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder) are borrowed from the wrong question.\\n- **PT101-S3-Q1**: the bank stimulus differs from the DB\'s in both speakers\' text (bank: election boards hiring extra help; Sharon concludes \\"apathy\\"). The DB text reads like the real question. The bank\'s choices for this question may not match either.\\n- 110 explained questions have no bank entry at all, so there is no difficulty and no answer-choice text on disk to check notes against (for example PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT131-S2-Q5).\\n\\n## 4. Empty or partial explanations\\n\\n| Problem | Count | Questions |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Explanation row exists with no steps and no notes | 2 | PT122-S1-Q9, PT147-S4-Q6 |\\n| Breakdown only, no choice notes | 16 | PT102-S3-Q14, PT117-S3-Q2, PT117-S3-Q8, PT117-S4-Q3, PT117-S4-Q5, PT118-S1-Q9, PT118-S3-Q8, PT119-S2-Q7, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S3-Q20, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S1-Q26, PT120-S4-Q17 |\\n| Some notes missing | 8 | PT105-S4-Q14 (3 of 5), PT133-S2-Q25 (3, including the correct answer), PT107-S3-Q7, PT108-S3-Q16, PT111-S1-Q22, PT119-S2-Q15, PT146-S3-Q11, PT156-S2-Q19 (4 each) |\\n| Placeholder text as a note | 2 | PT147-S4-Q4 A (correct), PT147-S4-Q3 D (correct): \\"(No authored tag or explanation for this choice.)\\" |\\n| Notes but no Argument Breakdown | 224 | Heaviest in PT148 (33), PT111 (14), PT122 (11), PT116 (10), PT102 (9). |\\n| Question has no explanation at all | 352 | Known (`is_explained = false`); the 772-gap plan covers these. |\\n\\nSeveral breakdown-only rows (PT117-S3-Q8, PT118-S1-Q9, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S4-Q17) are written in a different register from the LSAT Lab text: bracketed labels like \\"Rossi\'s argument structure\\", em-dashes, \\"i.e.\\" glosses. Use for lessons, notes, explanations, drills, quizzes, flashcards, and instructional feedback. Logistics emails and marketing use their separate canonical registers.\\n---\\n\\n# Write LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice\\n\\nResolve the canonical ai-os vault from the workspace instructions. This project-owned skill takes precedence over older vendor skills with the same name that prescribe a writer blend.\\n\\nBefore drafting or revising, read:\\n\\n1. [Canonical teaching voice](../../../wiki/teaching-voice.md), especially the teaching-content register.\\n2. [LSAT teaching voice standard](../../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). Read it in full when calibrating the voice; for subsequent revisions, revisit the relevant sections and examples.\\n3. [Demonstrated copy corrections](../../../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\n\\nThe detailed standard is maintained in one place. Do not copy it into this skill or replace it with generic brevity rules, required catchphrases, humor quotas, or a fixed number of sentences per paragraph.\\n\\nEstablish the actual lesson topic, prerequisites, evidence, and Joshua\'s approved method. The conditional reference teaches how to explain; it does not add conditional content to another topic. Preserve complete ideas, logical strength, modality, and the selected scope.\\n\\nUse original intuitive examples. Explain the ordinary meaning before notation, show the intermediate reasoning, and give questions a real instructional job. Keep the teacher and reader thinking together. When useful, show a tempting interpretation and how its meaning fails. Fresh practice should require the taught skill and provide reasoned feedback.\\n\\nRead the draft aloud, check its inferences and boundaries, and apply the standard\'s review rubric. Review actual prompt/reveal states when writing for an interactive surface. Do not claim an identical voice has been objectively established; use Joshua\'s corrections to refine calibration.\\n\\nThis skill governs local authoring. Posting, sending, assigning, deploying, or generating paid media requires the authorization and workflow appropriate to that action.\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"5ae9dc","wall_time_seconds":0.2876999,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":24,"output":"deferred-live-snapshot.json\\r\\ninitial-live-snapshot.json\\r\\nq157-styles-live.json\\r\\ntag-live.json\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 17.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 17468)\nTotal output lines: 4\n\n{"chunk_id":"b6bdf7","wall_time_seconds":0.3064979,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3303,"output":"---\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\ntype: concept\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\nstatus: active\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\naliases: []\\n---\\n\\n# Teaching Voice\\n\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\n\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\n\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\n\\n## Core voice (every register)\\n\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\n\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\n\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\n\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\n\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\n\\n## The apology / accountability move\\n\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\n\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\n\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\n\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\n\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\n\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\n\\n> Hi Sandra,\\n>\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\n>\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\n>\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\n> [link]\\n>\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\n>\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\n>\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\n>\\n> Best,\\n> Joshua\\n\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\n\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\n\\n## Situational micro-templates\\n\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\n\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\n\\n## The three registers\\n\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\n\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\n\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\n\\nReference line (target tone):\\n\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\n\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never \\"the reps aren\'t happening,\\" never tough love.\\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. \\"Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting.\\"\\n\\n### 2. Teaching content (conversational reasoning) -- lessons, recaps, notes, drills, explanations\\n\\nUse [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), Joshua\'s selected calibration from 2026-10-02. Keep the reader beside the teacher. Explain the ordinary meaning, ground it in an original example, make the small inferences audible, ask a useful question, and show both the consequence and its limit. Introduce terminology and notation when their meaning is established. Give each concept enough development to be learned from the page.\\n\\nContractions and varied sentence lengths should sound natural when read aloud. Repetition should change the reader\'s view of the same relationship. Questions should give the reader actual thinking work. Light humor may appear within an explanation when it helps understanding; this supersedes the older wit-only-at-the-edges restriction. Do not force punchlines, slang, hyperbole, catchphrases, or paragraph quotas.\\n\\nThe source governs delivery, not Joshua\'s procedure, indicator taxonomy, biography, or the scope of a lesson. Preserve his approved methods and logical precision. Personal encouragement still follows the earnest coaching register. Project-owned `lsat-u-voice` routes here and to the detailed standard; an older vendor writer-blend skill must not override this selection.\\n\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\n\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\n\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\n\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\n\\n## Don\'t\\n\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\n\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\n\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\n\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"fbc6dd","wall_time_seconds":0.2729193,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":14050,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 14050)\\nTotal output lines: 629\\n\\n---\\ntype: synthesis\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\nsources:\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\n---\\n\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\n\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\n\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\n\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\n\\n## Find the part you need\\n\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\n\\n## The evidence and coverage\\n\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\n\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\n\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\n\\n## The voice in one original passage\\n\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\n\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\n>\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\n>\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\n>\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\n>\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\n>\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\n>\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\n\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\n\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\n\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\n\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\n\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\n\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\n\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, ex…7468 tokens truncated…e student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize\\r\\nthe map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it\\r\\nexplains placement; it is not a fifth group.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five\\r\\nunlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to\\r\\nread.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching\\r\\nreminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of\\r\\nwhich answer they chose and how they explained it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a\\r\\nshort diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nA student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the\\r\\nexchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like\\r\\na compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the\\r\\ndetails make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a\\r\\ndurable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the\\r\\nconcept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\nwithout checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\nrelationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nthat the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nthem. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never\\r\\ninvent a fact to make a rule fire.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is\\r\\nexpected to remember, and a sign-off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced\\r\\nexamples, and practice that uses the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.\\r\\nIt must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of\\r\\nwhy the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each\\r\\npath in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a\\r\\nprocedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Define the concept in Joshua\'s language (2026-09-28)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”\\r\\ngrounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader\\r\\ndecode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks\\r\\nblurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a\\r\\nconcrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and\\r\\nthe inferences. Do not write an abstract umbrella sentence merely to announce the\\r\\ntopic.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Reference quality does not authorize reference topics (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua requested the Some, Most, and All note as a teaching-flow and visual-quality reference. Quantity examples and exercises do not belong in the separate facts/rules note. Remove that off-topic material; retain the distinction between what a rule says and what a fact establishes. Before future recaps, read the accumulated corrections in the Post Session Recap skill\'s `references/recap-calibration.md`. Preserve source-driven scope while matching the reference\'s teaching quality.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional practice: explain the decision instead of naming the operation (2026-10-07)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected “same rule, different words,” “equivalent-rule comparison,” “find the requirement, then place it,” “work through the expressions below,” “first choose the complete target,” “read the whole idea,” and “sentence X of Y” when these stood in for a useful explanation or made the drill feel like a collection of labeled sections. “Complete target” and similar terminology should not carry a beginner\'s instructions without a concrete meaning attached.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe revised local copy asks what the learner needs to decide: “Who does the rule say will receive a wristband?”, “What kind of key is needed to open the cabinet?”, and “What kind of pass does entry require?” The lesson demonstrates why before noon, current, registered and with yellow tags matter. Dropping one of those words changes which cases the rule covers. An arrow is checked by imagining its left condition and asking what the sentence then establishes, rather than treating the first English clause as the left side.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua also asked for the mixed drill to train a skill and build a habit, with one question visible and a progress bar. The current local response uses five stages with a concrete purpose for each: retain the full conditions, build a diagram, attempt a translation before supplied labels, compare meanings, then transfer to unfamiliar cases. Error feedback distinguishes dropped qualifications, reversed roles, required versus enough and changed meaning. A fresh question checks the repaired distinction; correcting the exposed answer alone does not demonstrate transfer. Written translations are compared by the learner and are not represented as automatically graded.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep modality in the examples. An electric vehicle that is denied permission disproves the claim that being electric guarantees permission; a vehicle merely staying outside does not. A required action is an obligation, not a fact proving compliance. Confirmation practice for that mistake must test another obligation rather than an unrelated wording comparison.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are demonstrated corrections and the current implementation response, not approval of every exact replacement sentence or proof that the voice matches Joshua identically. The [selected voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) still governs wording, cadence, concrete reasoning and scope. [Current session record](log.md) distinguishes local work from publication.\\r\\n\\r\\nFurther steering in the same session: “Keep the complete condition” was itself awkward as a visible heading. Ask who or what the particular rule covers instead. Joshua also rejected the landing page\'s total question/stage counts, per-stage counts, long stage list and padded explanation of repairs. A shorter launch should explain the immediate task; each stage should introduce its purpose and instructions when the learner reaches it. That latest flow revision was requested while final verification was still pending, so this record does not certify its implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\n### RC actions should describe what the student gets (2026-10-07)\\n\\nJoshua rejected Inspect set as unclear and awkward. Use Preview drill for viewing the exact selected passages and questions before starting. Keep Start this drill distinct and explain whether answers are hidden and whether work is saved. Generic Menu is also insufficient where the actual action is Question details. This correction does not justify adding more launch instructions, repeated summaries, or unrelated assigned work inside RC setup.\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"344ad8","wall_time_seconds":0.4553916,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2657,"output":"{\\n  \\"content\\": [\\n    {\\n      \\"type\\": \\"text\\",\\n      \\"text\\": \\"{\\\\n  \\\\\\"rows\\\\\\": [\\\\n    {\\\\n      \\\\\\"choice_d\\\\\\": [\\\\n        {\\\\n          \\\\\\"id\\\\\\": \\\\\\"75ee6f0d-1181-4f16-90d8-cb8c768d6e42\\\\\\",\\\\n          \\\\\\"letter\\\\\\": \\\\\\"D\\\\\\",\\\\n          \\\\\\"tag\\\\\\": \\\\\\"Strengthens\\\\\\",\\\\n          \\\\\\"text\\\\\\": \\\\\\"Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.\\\\\\"\\\\n        }\\\\n      ],\\\\n      \\\\\\"correct_choice\\\\\\": \\\\\\"A\\\\\\",\\\\n      \\\\\\"explanation_id\\\\\\": \\\\\\"b378e1b0-88e3-450f-9bdf-10644a048ea1\\\\\\",\\\\n      \\\\\\"notes_d\\\\\\": [\\\\n        {\\\\n          \\\\\\"body\\\\\\": \\\\\\"The placebo group was eating vitamin C-rich foods?
