[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 11.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 14646)\nTotal output lines: 2\n\n{"chunk_id":"389f78","wall_time_seconds":0.6144735,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":9365,"output":"\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that\'s the rule.” |\\r\\n| Inviting attention | let\'s look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |\\r\\n| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |\\r\\n| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |\\r\\n| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s browser corrections, 2026-10-03: define “guarantee” as the certainty supplied by the stated rule before using it to explain another idea. Explain “direction” through an actual relationship before using the word as advice. A beginner needs to see what the arrow means, which complete idea belongs on each side, and why sentence order need not match diagram order. “Read left to right” alone does not teach that. Introduce sufficient as enough and necessary as required through the example, then develop their limits.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor rule versus fact, make the hypothetical explicit: a rule tells us what would follow **if** a condition were true. It does not report that the condition happened. Contrast “Suppose this order is marked ready” with “This order is marked ready” before drawing a conclusion about an actual case. Keep the anchor sentence idiomatic and complete. Emphasis and motion around “if” should reveal that hypothetical relationship, with equally clear static information when motion is reduced.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntroduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nA repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition\'s shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.\\r\\n\\r\\nTransfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader\'s desire for certainty a recognizable shape.\\r\\n\\r\\nHumor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua\'s source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source\'s distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.\\r\\n\\r\\nNever turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua\'s biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision\\r\\n\\r\\nBold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.\\r\\n- Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.\\r\\n- Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.\\r\\n- Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.\\r\\n- Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.\\r\\n- Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.\\r\\n- Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The complete source progression and depth\\r\\n\\r\\nThis map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on infer…4646 tokens truncated…twice.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompanion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This\\r\\nsays what has actually gone wrong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nStarted 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\n\\r\\n2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Boxed `L -> R` → bold teaching notation **L → R** (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Concept lesson\\" → the actual reasoning title (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe recap preview exposed an internal label above the student lesson. Joshua explicitly rejected it. Use a title such as \\"Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\" and headings that teach the reasoning. Keep production labels, version metadata, and design commentary in private records. Check the rendered HTML, since its shell can introduce wording absent from the Markdown source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Recall Rephrase Colour\\" → \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\"\\r\\nThe door\'s heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line\\r\\nunder a chip reading \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\". \\"Rephrase\\" belongs to a different\\r\\nActivity, Conditional Rephrase. A test asserted the wrong name, which is why\\r\\n2,600 passing tests never caught it.\\r\\n**Lesson:** when a test pins a string, check the string is right, not just that\\r\\nit matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Intake only. Nothing comes back out yet…\\" → plain instruction\\r\\nClinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read \\"with full\\r\\nintonation\\" while step one is silent reading. The clause that made intonation\\r\\ninternal, \\"hear it naturally in your head\\", had been dropped in an earlier edit.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a word like \\"intake\\" reads as jargon. And when copy is trimmed,\\r\\ncheck what the trimmed clause was load-bearing for.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Three beats through the stimulus\\" → \\"Three steps\\"\\r\\n\\"Beat\\" is the internal code name for one of the four method steps. The glossary\\r\\nsays students see **step**. The interface ten pixels below labelled the same four\\r\\nthings Step 1 to Step 4, so the page used two names for one thing.\\r\\n**Lesson:** never let an implementation name reach the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"22 pieces to read\\" → line removed\\r\\nA **piece** is one chunk, roughly a sentence, and the instructions on that same\\r\\npage use the word that way. The count was of stimuli, so \\"22 pieces\\" read as 22\\r\\nsentences.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a unit word must mean one thing per surface. Check what a word\\r\\nalready means on the page before reusing it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"say it back\\" → \\"tell it back\\", \\"The Read\\" → \\"The Recall\\"\\r\\nThe library door still carried both retired names. \\"The Read\\" was step four\'s\\r\\nformer name and collided with step one, which is why it was renamed.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a rename is not finished until every row carries it. The door was\\r\\nstale for months because it is a different database row from the sections.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"then a fourth\\" → \\"then a fourth step\\"\\r\\nThe noun sat three words back and the reader had to carry it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"once you have got all of it\\" → \\"once you have read all of it\\"\\r\\nAmbiguous between having read it and having remembered it, in the one clause\\r\\nthat triggers step four.\\r\\n**Lesson:** precision matters most where the sentence is an instruction.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The three-fact notice → one fact\\r\\n\\"Nothing is recording yet, the microphone opens on Start, and the section\\r\\nrecords in one take that you cannot pause.\\" Two of those are said again in step\\r\\nfour and in the long version, so she skipped the paragraph.\\r\\n**Lesson:** say it once, in the place it matters.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The method, told twice → the long version collapsed\\r\\nThe page rendered four numbered steps and then the whole written briefing saying\\r\\nthe same thing at length, both open. She read the method twice and could not\\r\\ntell which one to follow.\\r\\n**Lesson:** two explanations of one thing is worse than either alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"PT105.S1 · 24 stimuli · about 25 min\\" → removed\\r\\nThe section was already the second line of the title. The clock is at the foot\\r\\nand in the long version. The stimulus count measured the work in a unit she has\\r\\nno feel for.\\r\\n**Lesson:** delete a line that repeats what is above it. Do not reword it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Booking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** \\"70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there\'s no rush to use them.\\"\\r\\n**Keep:** \\"You\'re set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSession length and expiry are admin fields. The student sees them on the booking\\r\\npage when she picks a time, so putting them in the email is detail she did not ask\\r\\nfor and has to read past to reach the link. The credit count is worth saying because\\r\\nit tells her how much she has. Everything else about the pass stays in the backend.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** in a booking email, say who it\'s for, how many sessions, and the link.\\r\\nStop there.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Drill hub: say what the button does, not what the feature is called (2026-09-09)\\r\\n\\r\\nSix changes on `/classroom/drill`, from the run-3 pass. The hub named its own\\r\\nfeatures, and it assumed the student had rejected something.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Was | Now |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| \\"Start Smart Drill\\" | \\"Practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start One Passage\\" | \\"Read one passage\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start a Smart Drill instead\\" | \\"Or practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Read one passage instead\\" | \\"Or read one passage now\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open analytics\\" | \\"See where you lose points\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open journal\\" | \\"Review your missed questions\\" |\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Smart Drill\\" is the product\'s internal name for the adaptive set. On a button it\\r\\nasks the student to guess what she is about to get. The sentence above the button\\r\\nstill names it, which is where a product name belongs: after the thing has been\\r\\nexplained, not as the explanation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Instead\\" was the worse one. It tells the student she has just declined the\\r\\nassigned work by reading past it. She has not. \\"Or\\" offers a second path without\\r\\nnarrating a decision she never made.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Open analytics\\" and \\"Open journal\\" name a screen. A student does not want\\r\\nanalytics, she wants to know where the points are going.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fallback paragraph lost \\"an adaptive set that follows your weakest question\\r\\ntypes\\" for \\"the question types you miss most\\": the same fact, without the product\\r\\nvocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** a button says what happens next, in the student\'s words. Feature names\\r\\nbelong in the sentence that explains them, never on the control.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Words never to use in student copy\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the glossary. Internal names may stay in code, never on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Never | Use instead |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| beat, beats | step |\\r\\n| chunk | piece |\\r\\n| intake | plain instruction |\\r\\n| briefing, phase, tab, card | the thing\'s own name |\\r\\n| say it back | tell it back |\\r\\n| The Read | The Recall |\\r\\n| Recall Rephrase Colour | Read, Recall, Colour |\\r\\n| em dash, en dash | a full stop, or a colon before a list |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conditional Rephrase: name the student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize\\r\\nthe map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it\\r\\nexplains placement; it is not a fifth group.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five\\r\\nunlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to\\r\\nread.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching\\r\\nreminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of\\r\\nwhich answer they chose and how they explained it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a\\r\\nshort diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nA student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the\\r\\nexchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like\\r\\na compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the\\r\\ndetails make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a\\r\\ndurable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the\\r\\nconcept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\nwithout checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\nrelationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nthat the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nthem. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never\\r\\ninvent a fact to make a rule fire.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is\\r\\nexpected to remember, and a sign-off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced\\r\\nexamples, and practice that uses the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.\\r\\nIt must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of\\r\\nwhy the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each\\r\\npath in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a\\r\\nprocedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Define the concept in Joshua\'s language (2026-09-28)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”\\r\\ngrounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader\\r\\ndecode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks\\r\\nblurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a\\r\\nconcrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and\\r\\nthe inferences. Do not write an abstract umbrella sentence merely to announce the\\r\\ntopic.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Reference quality does not authorize reference topics (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua requested the Some, Most, and All note as a teaching-flow and visual-quality reference. Quantity examples and exercises do not belong in the separate facts/rules note. Remove that off-topic material; retain the distinction between what a rule says and what a fact establishes. Before future recaps, read the accumulated corrections in the Post Session Recap skill\'s `references/recap-calibration.md`. Preserve source-driven scope while matching the reference\'s teaching quality.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional practice: explain the decision instead of naming the operation (2026-10-07)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected “same rule, different words,” “equivalent-rule comparison,” “find the requirement, then place it,” “work through the expressions below,” “first choose the complete target,” “read the whole idea,” and “sentence X of Y” when these stood in for a useful explanation or made the drill feel like a collection of labeled sections. “Complete target” and similar terminology should not carry a beginner\'s instructions without a concrete meaning attached.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe revised local copy asks what the learner needs to decide: “Who does the rule say will receive a wristband?”, “What kind of key is needed to open the cabinet?”, and “What kind of pass does entry require?” The lesson demonstrates why before noon, current, registered and with yellow tags matter. Dropping one of those words changes which cases the rule covers. An arrow is checked by imagining its left condition and asking what the sentence then establishes, rather than treating the first English clause as the left side.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua also asked for the mixed drill to train a skill and build a habit, with one question visible and a progress bar. The current local response uses five stages with a concrete purpose for each: retain the full conditions, build a diagram, attempt a translation before supplied labels, compare meanings, then transfer to unfamiliar cases. Error feedback distinguishes dropped qualifications, reversed roles, required versus enough and changed meaning. A fresh question checks the repaired distinction; correcting the exposed answer alone does not demonstrate transfer. Written translations are compared by the learner and are not represented as automatically graded.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep modality in the examples. An electric vehicle that is denied permission disproves the claim that being electric guarantees permission; a vehicle merely staying outside does not. A required action is an obligation, not a fact proving compliance. Confirmation practice for that mistake must test another obligation rather than an unrelated wording comparison.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are demonstrated corrections and the current implementation response, not approval of every exact replacement sentence or proof that the voice matches Joshua identically. The [selected voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) still governs wording, cadence, concrete reasoning and scope. [Current session record](log.md) distinguishes local work from publication.\\r\\n\\r\\nFurther steering in the same session: “Keep the complete condition” was itself awkward as a visible heading. Ask who or what the particular rule covers instead. Joshua also rejected the landing page\'s total question/stage counts, per-stage counts, long stage list and padded explanation of repairs. A shorter launch should explain the immediate task; each stage should introduce its purpose and instructions when the learner reaches it. That latest flow revision was requested while final verification was still pending, so this record does not certify its implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\n### RC actions should describe what the student gets (2026-10-07)\\n\\nJoshua rejected Inspect set as unclear and awkward. Use Preview drill for viewing the exact selected passages and questions before starting. Keep Start this drill distinct and explain whether answers are hidden and whether work is saved. Generic Menu is also insufficient where the actual action is Question details. This correction does not justify adding more launch instructions, repeated summaries, or unrelated assigned work inside RC setup.\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"Get-Content references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md | Select-Object -Skip 350","max_output_tokens":6500}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"b527a1","wall_time_seconds":0.3911018,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6622,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 6622)\\nTotal output lines: 278\\n\\n**Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ**, lines 390 to 433, has five questions:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Reader question | Depth of the answer | Reusable teaching value |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| When should I diagram? | Three paragraphs distinguish early training from later judgment, discuss difficult wording and chains, and address avoidance caused by uncertainty. | Give a criterion, a reason, and an honest account of the learning process. |\\r\\n| How do I know the diagram is correct? | Three paragraphs connect confidence with checked work and studying specific errors. | Teach a verification habit instead of offering confidence as a substitute for one. |\\r\\n| Does diagramming take too long? | Two paragraphs explain the proposed tradeoff and separate learning from speed. | Acknowledge the concern; explain why the slower practice step is useful now. |\\r\\n| Should I always write the contrapositive? | Two paragraphs qualify the advice. A separate note explains why the drills still require it. | Distinguish training evidence from the eventual performance habit. |\\r\\n| How should I abbreviate? | Gives a short example, a complete diagram, and advice about compact labels. | Make the notation choice purposeful and readable. Joshua\'s label guidance governs where it differs. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is unusually important to the overall tone. The writer gives practical uncertainty enough room to be answered. It is not squeezed into one generic reassurance.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 7. A useful shortcut is shown, then limited\\r\\n\\r\\n**Figuring Out The Language**, lines 437 to 471, defines an indicator\'s target and brackets the target in familiar sentences. It first shows the common placement immediately after the indicator. Then it gives five examples in which the target comes before the indicator, followed by three paired wording variations showing that the same indicator can switch position.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sequence deliberately prevents an attractive mechanical generalization. The reader sees why adjacency often helps and why it cannot replace reading the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** teach the indicator\'s complete target. Change clause order while holding meaning steady. Include one placement trap only after the usual case is understood. Mark the obscured final line as missing evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 8. A translation is checked by trying both interpretations\\r\\n\\r\\n**Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then**, lines 475 to 508, defines the check, explains its purpose, gives four steps, establishes the ordinary meaning of a new sentence, and works through a correct guess. It then deliberately tries the reversed guess and explains why that meaning fails.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis adds a verification strategy after notation. It also models the experience of being uncertain without abandoning the task. The teacher does not merely say “use your intuition”; the reader sees how to test an interpretation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** use the imagined situation as a meaning check. Preserve who or what the sentence concerns. Personalization is optional if pretending to be an object makes the wording less natural.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 9. Practice changes the language while keeping the skill\\r\\n\\r\\n**Basic Conditional Drill**, lines 512 to 563, gives supportive instructions and six themed items, with original and contrapositive diagrams displayed in the supplied HTML. The task is the same across varied sentence forms. The source says the adapted sentences come from Shakespeare and points to an answer key not included here.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** original practice can have a memorable theme, but it should change only what the learner is ready to handle. Reuse the skill, not the source\'s exercise wording. Show answers after an attempt and explain why each diagram preserves the complete ideas. Do not treat the displayed annotations as an independently verified answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe six items do not cover every expression in the source\'s own indicator tables. In particular, they do not independently train only, only if, depends, or most other necessary expressions. Their displayed diagrams also lack step-by-step explanations. Our Group 1/2 practice needs broader expression coverage and reasoned feedback; the source\'s basic set alone would not establish that coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp\\r\\n\\r\\n**Joshua\'s instruction on 2026-10-03 changes the conditional curriculum scope and sequencing.** The complete supplied HTML now sets its content-order backbone, as well as its voice: if/then meaning; sufficient and its word family; necessary and its word family; conceptual contrapositive; four situations; diagramming and symbolic contraposition; practical FAQ; indicator targets; Personalized If/Then; integrated basic practice. Preserve every supplied concept in that relative order, with original examples and additional depth. The [complete ordered skeleton and integration map](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) is the current authoring plan.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis explicitly includes a conceptual contrapositive before notation and supersedes the earlier restriction to the first four opening units. Existing code does not become sequence-compliant by being linked to this plan. Keep the source\'s logical simplifications bounded, preserve Joshua\'s five-step method and contextual group definitions, and preserve assignment and historical-answer compatibility when implementing the sequence. This source-specific curriculum order does not impose conditional topics on unrelated LSAT lessons.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following rows record the earlier opening-only planning model and useful concept/practice coverage. Their arrangement is **not the current teaching sequence**. Reuse their material in the new ordered skeleton rather than retaining their old lesson order.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |\\r\\n| **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |\\r\\n| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |\\r\\n| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\\r\\n| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\\r\\n| **Group 1** | Joshua\'s Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\\r\\n| **Group 2** | Joshua\'s Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression\'s target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua\'s assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give an idea several teachable moments\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve the real indicator groups\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current reviewed opening materials use:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n\\r\\nSee the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source\'s two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua\'s notes and scope.\\r\\n\\r\\n“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve Joshua\'s five steps\\r\\n\\r\\nIn [Sandra\'s 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua\'s five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Carry these improvements forward\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\\r\\n2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\\r\\n3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\\r\\n4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\\r\\n5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\\r\\n6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\\r\\n7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\\r\\n8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\\r\\n9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\\r\\n10. **Keep each operation behind its prerequisites.** Under the 2026-10-03 sequence, conceptual contraposition follows both condition roles and families, and precedes notation. Chains, Groups 3/4 and quantities still receive later development. The source does not contain those complete later curricula.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | …122 tokens truncated…lent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\\r\\n| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\\r\\n| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\\r\\n| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua\'s clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\\r\\n| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\\r\\n| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\\r\\n| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence\'s subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\\r\\n| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\\r\\n| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author\'s advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |\\r\\n| References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, or named source tools. | Preserve the teaching function only where our course has an actual equivalent. Do not import obsolete or unavailable context. |\\r\\n| A theme makes a drill memorable. | Keep each sentence logically interpretable and its answer reviewed. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.\\r\\n- Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.\\r\\n- Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces\\r\\n\\r\\nAll examples below are original drafts for this reference. They demonstrate writing mechanisms; they are not assigned work or approved curriculum.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain sufficient and necessary without rushing\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose yours has a blue sticker. That\'s enough to know it has been inspected. You don\'t need another fact about the parcel. The rule has already told you what must be true of every parcel with that sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We call the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We still don\'t know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning\\r\\n\\r\\n> Editing this newsletter requires an account.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Front**\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Back**\\r\\n\\r\\n> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n\\r\\n> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n\\r\\n> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n>\\r\\n> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n\\r\\n### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n\\r\\nList what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n\\r\\nRead as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n\\r\\nPut the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nRead aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n\\r\\nScore each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nWrite this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\nRead wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\nand wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\nthe idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\nShow each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\nwould actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\nroom for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nVary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\nsentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\nTeach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\nRepeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\nShow a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\nand the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\nDo not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\ncounts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\nor decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\nprompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\nfollows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keep this reference current\\r\\n\\r\\nThis file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua\'s later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source archive is immutable. Future evidence or revised attachments should be recorded as a separate generation with provenance, not written over the original.\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nimport pathlib,json,hashlib,re\np=pathlib.Path('corpus/lr-explanations/fixes/step4-2026-10-10');data=json.loads((p/'q157-styles-live.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))['structuredContent']['rows'];newtext={'brief':\"Vitamin C in the placebo group's food could blur the comparison, which weakens rather than supports the conclusion. But D only says some subjects ate those foods. We don't know the dose or whether those subjects were in the blind comparison. A gives stronger evidence: the vitamin C group had a history of worse acne.\",'another_way':\"Suppose vitamin C does help, but some placebo subjects get it from food. Their improvement could make the groups look alike even though the vitamin works. That would undermine the conclusion, not support it.\\n\\nD leaves too much open, though. How much vitamin C did those subjects get? Were they among the subjects who couldn't tell which pill they had? The answer doesn't say. A gives a clearer reason the comparison could hide a benefit: the vitamin C group had a history of worse acne.\"};proposals=[]\nfor row in data:\n if row['style'] not in newtext:continue\n n=row['notes_d'][0]; new=newtext[row['style']]; assert not re.search(r'\\b(he|she|his|her|him|hers|himself|herself)\\b|[—–]',new,re.I)\n proposals.append({'qid':row['qid'],'table':'lr_explanation_choice_notes','column':'body','row_id':n['id'],'explanation_id':row['explanation_id'],'style':row['style'],'letter':'D','old':n['body'],'old_md5':n['md5'],'new':new,'new_md5':hashlib.md5(new.encode()).hexdigest(),'rationale':'Dietary vitamin C in a control group can contaminate the comparison; it does not strengthen a conclusion of no real benefit. Limited quantifier/dose/blind-subgroup information makes D a weaker objection than A.','source':'q157-styles-live.json'})\nc=data[0]['choice_d'][0];proposals.append({'qid':'PT157-S3-Q25','table':'lr_answer_choices','column':'tag','row_id':c['id'],'letter':'D','old':c['tag'],'old_md5':hashlib.md5(c['tag'].encode()).hexdigest(),'new':'Too Weak','new_md5':hashlib.md5(b'Too Weak').hexdigest(),'rationale':'Potential control contamination weakens rather than strengthens, but D supplies less decisive evidence than credited A.','source':'q157-styles-live.json'})\n(p/'proposals.json').write_bytes((json.dumps(proposals,ensure_ascii=False,indent=2)+'\\n').encode());print('3 proposed edits; standard preserved')\n'@ | python -I -","max_output_tokens":800}));

\\"f8dd1284-4a93-4182-a439-f0a28e2b7926\\", \\"letter\\": \\"A\\", \\"md5\\": \\"8f9385a97ef4dfa185b3fa5fbfac5c84\\", \\"text\\": \\"some of Professor O’Brien’s students are very interested in Mercado’s work but were unable to attend the lecture\\"}, {\\"id\\": \\"213470d4-99f0-4dc0-91e3-67f58d666664\\", \\"letter\\": \\"B\\", \\"md5\\": \\"6307240cf6ccba66e33d2d57375bc0ac\\", \\"text\\": \\"many people who are not Professor O’Brien’s students did not think that the lecture was fascinating, even though they heard it from the beginning\\"}, {\\"id\\": the lecture and did not find the lecture fascinating\\"}, {\\"id\\": \\"c93ae381-430b-42ad-88a0-807d7544bb91\\", \\"letter\\": \\"D\\", \\"md5\\": \\"fde4012bc158c539a4cc8c20e615c733\\", \\"text\\": \\"no one who heard the lecture from the beginning and thought that it was fascinating was one of Professor O’Brien’s students\\"}, {\\"id\\": \\"8a37c5f8-8a93-4443-b7d1-945e3dbb2a55\\", \\"letter\\": \\"E\\", \\"md5\\": \\"d40f9338e0af0ba48d9cf4fd788a6b17\\", \\"text\\": \\"not everyone who arrived at the lecture on time was able to hear the entire lecture because of disruptions caused by those who were late in arriving\\"}], \\"correct_choice\\": \\"D\\", \\"id\\": \\"140a4350-6f6e-4eb1-a1b2-6adc1ae60e83\\", \\"prompt\\": \\"Professor O’Brien’s argument does not follow logically from its premises because of the possibility that\\", \\"qid\\": \\"PT159-S1-Q21\\", \\"question_type\\": \\"Flaw\\", \\"stimulus\\": \\"Professor O’Brien: Any of my students who heard Mercado’s lecture from the begi\\r\\nnewlineRows <class \'dict\'>\\r\\n{\\"rows\\": [{\\"body\\": \\"This isn\'t a true paradox. Nothing here is surprising. The stem just wants explanations for a drop in the number of bass caught.\\\\r\\\\n\\\\r\\\\nThink about what \\\\\\"number of bass caught\\\\\\" depends on. It takes two things: bass in the water and people fishing for them. So there are two roads to fewer bass caught:\\\\r\\\\n1. **Fewer bass downstream.** They died, got eaten, moved away, or lost their habitat.\\\\r\\\\n2. **Less fishing downstream.** Fewer anglers, or anglers fishing less.\\\\r\\\\n\\\\r\\\\nExpect most of the answers we eliminate to come in on road 1, with at least one sneaking in on road 2.\\\\r\\\\n\\\\r\\\\nMost students only think of road 1, because the wastewater feels like it must be hurting the fish. But road 2 is just as good. If half the anglers stop showing up, half as many bass get caught, even if the bass are thriving.\\", \\"id\\": \\"30050c0b-3c74-407f-a7ab-42bd91a5178d\\", \\"label\\": \\"Evaluate\\", \\"new_md5\\": \\"1ccdbd3ca089eac750b68e5cf4223598\\", \\"old_md5\\": \\"c9fe331374bbbe17c7ac3f9ed1a95db6\\", \\"position\\": 2, \\"qid\\": \\"PT108-S3-Q6\\"}, {\\"body\\": \\"Eliminate four answers that explain fewer bass caught, by either road. The correct answer will do neither, or it will push the other way: more bass or more fishing, which should mean *more* bass caught.\\\\r\\\\n\\\\r\\\\nBe careful with any answer about anglers. It isn\'t out of scope. Anglers are half the equation.\\", \\"id\\": \\"3a915989-8077-4179-8156-11839c6a5952\\", \\"label\\": \\"Goal\\", \\"new_md5\\": \\"2a912b4ff39edb7a272df9230230e31a\\", \\"old_md5\\": \\"3c26722b61516a64cf1806abf64423e2\\", \\"position\\": 3, \\"qid\\": \\"PT108-S3-Q6\\"}]}\\r\\n"}'}]

pathlib,json,hashlib\np=pathlib.Path('corpus/lr-explanations/fixes/step4-2026-10-10');x=json.loads((p/'initial-live-snapshot.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8')); edits=json.loads((p/'proposals.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8')); md=lambda s:hashlib.md5(s.encode()).hexdigest(); changes={'PT108-S2-Q18':[('grass:', 'grass.')],'PT141-S2-Q5':[('Lemaitre','Lemaître'),('primeval in atom','primeval atom')],'PT143-S1-Q13':[('Bums','Burns')],'PT143-S4-Q1':[('Trinidadians','Trinidadian')]}\nfor row in x['sources']['structuredContent']['rows']:\n if row['qid'] not in changes:continue\n old=row['stimulus'];new=old\n for a,b in changes[row['qid']]:\n  assert a in new,(row['qid'],a);new=new.replace(a,b)\n edits.append({'qid':row['qid'],'table':'lr_questions','column':'stimulus','row_id':row['id'],'old':old,'old_md5':md(old),'new':new,'new_md5':md(new),'rationale':'Minimal transcription repairs confirmed against published bank and source review.','replacements':changes[row['qid']],'source':'initial-live-snapshot.json + live-bank-source.json'})\nfor row in x['newlineRows']['structuredContent']['rows']:\n old=row['body'];new=old.replace('\\r\\n','\\n');assert md(old)==row['old_md5'];assert md(new)==row['new_md5']\n edits.append({'qid':row['qid'],'table':'lr_explanation_steps','column':'body','row_id':row['id'],'explanation_id':'7aca97bc-3629-4277-bbd4-5597bdda2c4c','position':row['position'],'label':row['label'],'old':old,'old_md5':md(old),'new':new,'new_md5':md(new),'rationale':'Normalize actual CRLF paragraph breaks to LF, preserving every visible character.','source':'initial-live-snapshot.json'})\nrow=json.loads((p/'tag-live.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))['structuredContent']['rows'][0];new='Not a Case of \"Reliable Process\"';edits.append({'qid':row['qid'],'table':'lr_answer_choices','column':'tag','row_id':row['id'],'question_id':row['question_id'],'letter':row['letter'],'old':row['tag'],'old_md5':row['tag_md5'],'new':new,'new_md5':md(new),'rationale':'Remove escaped quote and level metadata parser artifacts without changing tag meaning.','source':'tag-live.json'})\n(p/'payload-candidate.json').write_bytes((json.dumps(edits,ensure_ascii=False,indent=2)+'\\n').encode('utf-8'));print(len(edits),'candidate edits; source statuses pending independent review')\n'@ | python -I -","max_output_tokens":1000}));
